### Conditions of entry — the gate on the first invite (new IDs)

`SUMMARY.md` §4 states these as a literal gate on the first Telegram invite: all seven hold, or
no invite is sent. Every named precedent in this domain — Coinhive, The Pirate Bay,
Showtime/CBS, BrowseAloud/ICO.gov.uk — has the same shape: CPU was used without telling the
visitor, a third party found it, and the finding cost far more trust than the cycles were worth.

**Two of these look like re-mints of a closed v1 row and are not**, and the distinction is the
same one v1.1's preamble drew when it refused to mint `WIRE-05` beside `DATA-03`. `BROW-01` and
`BROW-04` are closed and stay closed; each new row below states the obligation that is
materially stricter than the row it sits beside, and it is the stricter obligation that is
unmet — not the original one restated.

- [ ] **BROW-06**: A visitor's opt-in blocks the artifact **fetch**, not only execution — no
      task bytes are requested before consent is recorded. **Not a re-mint of BROW-01**, which
      is satisfied by consent before compute begins and stays closed: this row moves the line
      earlier, because a fetch on its own reads as preparation-to-run to a reviewer watching
      network traffic, and the reviewer is the audience this gate exists for
- [ ] **BROW-07**: A persistent indicator says the tab is computing, and it is visible while
      the tab is **unfocused** — a tab-title glyph or equivalent, not page-body content alone —
      for as long as any task is in flight
- [ ] **BROW-08**: The stop control is a hard interrupt of the `Worker.terminate()` class:
      CPU is measured falling to zero, and no in-flight task is allowed to run to completion.
      **Not a re-mint of BROW-04**, which a cooperative "stop accepting new tasks" satisfies
      and this row refuses. The connection the tab held to the hosted tier closes with it, so
      the duration billing that connection was accruing stops too — on this platform cost is
      held sockets, not messages, so a stop that leaves the socket open has not stopped
      anything the operator pays for
- [ ] **BROW-09**: Plain-language disclosure is shown **before** opt-in and states four things:
      what code runs, whose task it is, what leaves the device — where the sovereignty
      guarantee is a selling point rather than a caveat — and what telemetry is sent
- [ ] **BROW-10**: A rough data-cost disclosure sits alongside the CPU disclosure and before
      opt-in, stated in bytes for a representative task, because an international cohort has a
      mobile-data subset
- [ ] **RUN-01**: No recruitment invite is sent until `BROW-06`…`BROW-10`, `RUN-02` and
      `RUN-03` all hold, recorded as a dated go/no-go checklist naming each condition's
      evidence. This is a gate, not a nice-to-have: **a Telegram-recruited cohort of a few
      hundred is spendable exactly once**, and SETI@home's move to BOINC lost roughly half of
      its ~600,000 volunteers to added platform complexity alone — no bug, no bad actor
- [ ] **RUN-02**: A kill switch stops the cohort admitting new tasks with **no redeploy**,
      sliceable by region and by client version rather than global-only, and verifiable by a
      volunteer rather than only in internal telemetry. **Open question 2 changes this row**:
      whether Workers KV's ~60 s global propagation is acceptable, or the push-over-an-open-socket
      path must ship in the same phase, is unsettled
- [ ] **RUN-03**: A minimal status page is reachable by a volunteer before the invite is sent.
      A read-only view over data the fabric already publishes — DHT records, benchmark output —
      is enough; what is not enough is nothing

---

### The hosted always-on tier (new prefix `HOST-`)

Three roles were measured working on one deployed Durable Object on 2026-08-24
(`.planning/consults/2026-08-24-cloudflare-as-a-fabric-node-measured.md` §7, §9, §13). These
rows require them **shipped in this repository**, which is a different thing from having been
measured in a consult. The structural shape is settled: a third assembly file in a third
workspace package, `packages/cloudflare/`, beside `packages/browser` and `packages/node` — not
a branch inside `fabric-node.ts`.

- [x] **HOST-01**: A deployed hosted node is dialable as an ordinary libp2p peer over WSS at
      `/dns4/<name>/tcp/443/tls/ws/p2p/<peerId>`, and its identity is persisted in that
      object's own storage and unchanged across eviction and across redeploy — a peer that
      dials it twice, days apart, gets the same PeerId
- [ ] **HOST-02**: The same node runs a Circuit Relay v2 server: a browser reserves a slot,
      a second browser reaches the first **only** through it, verifies the first's PeerId, and
      the pair completes a WebRTC handshake after which the relay leaves the data path. It
      declares `addresses.announce` — with nothing declared the server has no address to hand
      a client and every reservation comes back empty
- [x] **HOST-03**: The same node holds and answers records on the fabric's private keyspace
      `/o2/kad/1.0.0`, configured with the four settings a private DHT needs rather than two:
      `protocol`, `clientMode`, `peerInfoMapper` and `selectors`
- [x] **HOST-04**: A Cloudflare-hosted node **never advertises execution capability**. Its
      published `NodeRecords.capabilities` (`packages/core/src/discovery.ts:414`) omits it and
      a guard fails if an execution capability ever appears in one, so the scheduler never
      learns the hosted tier exists as anything but a capability-limited participant. Runtime
      WASM compilation is refused there on every entry point by a V8 embedder flag — the same
      one that disables `eval` — and `WebAssembly.instantiateStreaming` does not exist at all.
      The requirement is the omission, not a workaround for the refusal
- [x] **HOST-05**: Durable Object storage is reached through `interface-datastore` by a
      hand-written class in this repository, following `packages/node/src/fs-datastore.ts`.
      **No published package binds `interface-datastore` to Durable Object storage**, and the
      last generic async datastore this project reached for (`datastore-level`) hung the
      enrollment RPC for a week — which is why `fs-datastore.ts` exists at all
- [ ] **HOST-06**: The three regions are `bootstrap-us`, `bootstrap-eu` and `bootstrap-sam`,
      one identity in one object each. `bootstrap-eu` is created in the **`eu` jurisdiction**,
      which is binding. `bootstrap-sam` is a **`locationHint` only**, because no
      South-American jurisdiction value exists — `jurisdiction` takes `eu`, `us` and
      `fedramp` and nothing else
- [ ] **HOST-07**: No surface, document or published record claims **where** a hosted object
      actually runs. `locationHint` is best-effort: the object is placed in a datacenter chosen
      to minimise latency *from* the hint, so the region name is an address and never a
      location claim. A report that says "measured in São Paulo" on the strength of a hint has
      written a measured fact it did not measure
- [x] **HOST-08**: Exactly **one** call site in the repository may create these objects, and a
      guard fails on a second. An object's location is fixed by its very first `get()` and
      never changes afterwards, so a stray `get()` from anywhere else permanently sites the
      object wherever that call came from
- [x] **HOST-09**: `getAlarm()` is checked before every `setAlarm()`, and a minimum reschedule
      interval is enforced so an alarm cannot reschedule itself tighter than that floor
- [ ] **HOST-10**: A billing alert is configured **before the first Durable Object is
      deployed**. What is independently corroborated is that Cloudflare has **no hard spending
      limit** — its budget alerts are, in its own words, *"informational only. It does not cap
      your usage."* The runaway-alarm cost figure usually quoted beside this is **one Hacker
      News self-report** (`thewillmoss`, 2026-04-16, 31 points, 4 comments, no billing
      response), was for ~930 billion row reads per day rather than for alarm invocations, and
      was multiplied by 60+ preview deployments. It is cited with those qualifiers or not at all
- [x] **HOST-11**: The hosted tier has **no preview deployments, ever** — a deploy
      configuration or CI path that would create one fails a guard. The preview multiplier is
      the part of the self-report in `HOST-10` that is structural rather than anecdotal: the
      per-deployment cost was ordinary and the count was not
- [x] **HOST-12**: The `idFromName()` name set is closed, short, and enumerated in source. No
      name is ever derived from visitor-controlled input, so no visitor can cause an object to
      be created
- [x] **HOST-13**: The provider-record validity policy this project **already ships** runs on
      the hosted tier, driven by a **Durable Object alarm**. `providerRecordPolicy`
      (`packages/libp2p/src/constants.ts:316`) derives sweep interval and republish threshold
      from a single validity of one hour; both tiers pass it
      (`packages/node/src/fabric-node.ts:2154`, `packages/browser/src/browser-node.ts:1539`);
      and it is read across two processes by `packages/node/src/provider-expiry.node.test.ts`
      under NET-06. It is driven by a `setInterval`, which will not fire reliably on workerd
      because `Date.now()` does not advance without I/O. **The work is the driver, not the
      policy — this row is a port, and a plan that rebuilds the policy has done the wrong job**
- [x] **HOST-14**: `/o2/<nodeKey>` value records expire by a sweep, and the sweep is observed
      running against **real, not synthetic** records **before** persistence ships to the
      public cohort. Durable Object storage is durable by construction, unlike the browser's
      evictable IndexedDB, so this is the first tier where records that never expire simply
      accumulate. A sweep that ships after persistence has already lost the property
- [x] **HOST-15**: The hosted node's socket is written against the hibernation API and gives a
      defined answer for the absent `bufferedAmount`. Billing on this platform is duration of
      held sockets rather than messages carried, so a non-hibernating socket is the cost, and a
      backpressure signal that silently reads `undefined` is a correctness defect that looks
      like a healthy node

---

### Transport, fallback, and the peer-to-peer / relay split (existing `NET-` prefix)

- [x] **NET-11**: The hosted tier's inbound listener declares `direction: 'inbound'` and
      derives each connection's remote address from `CF-Connecting-IP`. **Both fail silently
      when omitted**, and without the second libp2p treats the whole internet as one host and
      caps admission at five connections per second. The verification uses **more than five**
      concurrent distinct client addresses — one or two peers cannot surface this defect by
      construction, so a green from a two-peer test is a green that could not have gone red
- [ ] **NET-12**: TURN is the first fallback rung below direct WebRTC, with short-lived
      credentials supplied through `WebRTCTransportInit.rtcConfiguration`'s function form,
      sharded to match the three bootstrap regions, and with credential rotation and
      open-relay hardening verified before the public run. Cross-continent pairs concentrate
      exactly where TURN is absent, which is why the sharding is part of the requirement.
      **Open question 4 changes this row and it deliberately names no provider**: where TURN
      runs is unmeasured in both 2026-08-24 consults and is the one point where the four v2.0
      research files disagree with each other
- [x] **NET-13**: A relayed connection is the second fallback rung, and the protocol
      specification states its budget **per direction**: the Circuit Relay v2 data limit is
      enforced **bidirectionally**, so a symmetric request/response protocol gets **64 KiB each
      way, not 128**. A boundary test exercises the **relayed** path, not only direct WebRTC —
      a design that assumes twice the room has assumed it from a figure that reads as
      one-directional and is not
- [x] **NET-14**: Two counters are reported from the first day the hosted tier is live:
      connection-seconds and bytes carried **peer-to-peer**, against connection-seconds and
      bytes carried **through the relay**. This is structural, not instrumentation polish —
      the hosted tier becoming load-bearing while every document still says peer-to-peer is
      the **median** outcome for hosted-relay systems, with IPFS's measured cloud reliance
      (arXiv:2309.16203) and Matrix's homeserver dominance as the two precedents
- [ ] **NET-15**: A **scheduled, repeated** relay-kill drill takes the hosted relay out and
      reports bounded, measured degradation rather than a silent outage. Scheduled and
      repeated is the requirement; a one-time design review is what this replaces

---

### The public run and what it measures (new prefix `RUN-`)

- [ ] **RUN-04**: The six-stage connectivity funnel — page load → consent → WSS to bootstrap →
      ICE gathering → connection classified → first task executed — is instrumented and
      reporting live **before** recruitment begins. **No published figure exists for what
      fraction of a general audience cannot participate.** The bounds available are proxies and
      are labelled as proxies wherever quoted: 10–20% relay-required industry guidance, and
      70%±7.1% DCUtR hole-punch success with ~30% relay fallback and 11% symmetric NAT from a
      *different* protocol (arXiv:2510.27500). This milestone is how the real number gets taken
- [ ] **RUN-05**: The telemetry schema is designed backward from exactly three questions — the
      scaling curve, the WebRTC failure rate by country and network class, and diurnal churn —
      is **frozen before recruitment**, is aggregate-only, and discards raw IP at collection.
      **Open question 3 changes this row**: whether the legal basis is consent or legitimate
      interest is contested across sources rather than merely unresolved. The engineering
      recommendation — carry telemetry consent on the same gate as `BROW-06` rather than
      showing a second banner — is a **design recommendation, not a compliance ruling**, and is
      flagged for legal review before shipping
- [ ] **RUN-06**: Telegram's in-app WebView is detected and the visitor is offered an "open in
      your own browser" interstitial, verified by opening the **real** recruitment link from a
      **real** Telegram message on **both iOS and Android** before recruiting begins. **Open
      question 5 changes this row**: whether that WebView suspends JS on backgrounding, and
      whether it diverges on IndexedDB or WebRTC, is a MEDIUM-confidence general WebView
      pattern and was not found in any authoritative current source for Telegram specifically
- [ ] **RUN-07**: The cohort is invited in stages — by region and by cohort slice — rather than
      all at once, and each stage's go/no-go reads `RUN-04`'s funnel before the next invite is
      sent. **No named volunteer-computing precedent exists for staged rollout of a compute
      cohort**; this is inferred from general release practice and is recorded as inferred
      rather than sourced

---

### Published numbers only the run can produce (existing `BENCH-` prefix)

Both rows below inherit the discipline the existing `BENCH-` family already carries:
pre-registered methodology before the first published number (`BENCH-02`) and percentiles
rather than means (`BENCH-03`).

- [ ] **BENCH-08**: The WebRTC connection-failure rate is published **segmented by country and
      by network class**, with `RUN-04`'s page-load stage as the denominator, and is stated as
      measured — never merged with, backfilled from, or presented beside the proxy figures in
      `RUN-04` in a way that lets a reader take a proxy for a measurement
- [ ] **BENCH-09**: A diurnal churn curve is published from the run — node arrivals and
      departures against time of day, per region — with the observation window and the
      population size stated beside it

---

### Disclosure and contribution posture (existing `DEMO-` prefix)

- [x] **DEMO-05**: `CONTRIBUTING.md` states explicitly that pull requests are **triaged, never
      merged**, and that any fix is implemented **independently of the reported diff**. No CLA
      is planned (owner ruling, 2026-08-24 — rely on the civilized world rather than build CLA
      machinery), so provenance for the later relicensing track is preserved by not merging
      rather than by paperwork. Monetizing commercial use later requires owning every line
- [x] **DEMO-06**: Public recruitment copy and project copy do not promise permanent open
      licensing. The settled position is open source **with monetization for commercial use
      added later**, and three dated precedents — Terraform→OpenTofu, Redis→Valkey,
      Elastic→OpenSearch — show the shape of the backlash when the copy and the later licensing
      disagree

---

### Open questions — these change a requirement and are not settled

Recorded, not answered. Each names what would settle it and which rows move when it does.

1. **SETTLED 2026-08-25 by measurement, and the question turned out to be the wrong question.**
   It read: *can wrangler's `alias` redirect one deep file inside `@chainsafe/libp2p-noise`, or
   whole packages only* — as the fix for the `node:crypto.diffieHellman` gap. **No alias is
   needed at all.** Wrangler's default resolution already honours the package's legacy top-level
   `browser` field and bundles `index.browser.ts`, so `diffieHellman` never enters the bundle,
   with and without `nodejs_compat`, on both wrangler versions tested. Established by causation
   rather than correlation: deleting the `browser` field from a *private scratch copy* flips the
   build to the Node path. The literal question also has an answer — deep aliasing works only by
   the specifier string **exactly as written in the importing module** (`./crypto/index.js`),
   while every package-qualified form is a **silent no-op** that builds green with a bundle
   identical to the baseline; and because that key is a relative specifier it matches across the
   whole dependency graph, silently replacing any other module importing the same string.
   Runtime was checked as well, locally and without deploying: the baseline completes a
   handshake, the Node backend planted returns
   `TypeError: crypto2.diffieHellman is not a function`.
   **Changes `HOST-01`:** it ships no alias line, and gains a guard asserting `diffieHellman` is
   absent from the emitted bundle — cheap, and red exactly when this silently regresses. A second
   finding neither investigator was asked for: **wrangler is declared in no `package.json` in
   this repository**, so `npx wrangler` resolves to whatever is ambient (4.14.1 here, against a
   4.125.0 pin); Phase 29 pins it. Full working, **including what it does not establish** — the
   full Phase 29 graph and the `ws` CJS-dynamic-require question are untested, and "moot" is a
   claim about this one file's resolution, not about the hosted tier bundling:
   `.planning/consults/2026-08-25-noise-diffiehellman-on-workerd-measured.md`.
2. **Is Workers KV's ~60 s global propagation acceptable for the kill switch, or must the
   push-over-an-open-socket path ship in the same phase?** The cheap version is a KV poll with
   a Durable Object broadcast layered on only if the sub-minute window proves unacceptable in
   practice. **Settled by:** whether "a stop control that provably drops CPU to zero" is read as
   node-local — the client reads its own flag, which `STACK.md` argues matters more — or as a
   global propagation-latency bound. **Changes:** `RUN-02`, and how `BROW-08` is read.
3. **Telemetry legal basis: consent versus legitimate interest under GDPR.** **Contested across
   sources, not merely unresolved** — the sources consulted disagreed with each other on
   whether IP-based analytics needs consent or can rest on legitimate interest. **Settled by:
   legal review, not engineering judgement.** The engineering recommendation (carry telemetry
   consent on the same gate as the CPU-use consent rather than a second banner) is explicitly a
   **design recommendation and not a compliance ruling**, and is flagged for legal review
   before shipping. **Changes:** `RUN-05`, `BROW-09`.
4. **Where does TURN run?** Unmeasured in both 2026-08-24 consults, and the one point where the
   four v2.0 research files disagree with each other: `STACK.md` recommends Cloudflare Realtime
   TURN specifically to avoid a second vendor relationship and a second always-up server — the
   things the hosted-tier decision existed to avoid — while `ARCHITECTURE.md` calls for a
   dedicated research spike instead. **Settled by:** that spike. **Changes:** `NET-12`, which
   is why it names no provider.
5. **Does Telegram's in-app WebView kill sessions on backgrounding, and does it diverge on
   IndexedDB or WebRTC?** A MEDIUM-confidence general WebView pattern; Telegram's own current
   behaviour was not found in an authoritative current source. **Settled by:** opening the
   actual recruitment link from a real Telegram message on both iOS and Android before
   recruiting begins — cheap to check, expensive to get wrong at cohort scale. **Changes:**
   `RUN-06`, and `RUN-01`'s gate.
6. **`DEMO-04`'s guard — SETTLED 2026-08-25 by owner ruling; kept here rather than deleted,
   because a question that was open is part of the record.** It read: the guard
   (`packages/node/src/disclosure-gate.node.test.ts`) forbids a deploy workflow in order to
   prevent an irreversible legal event, and that event is now the plan, so retire / repurpose /
   keep was an open decision. **The ruling is repurpose.** The mechanism stays; its rationale is
   rewritten from *disclosure is irreversible* to *deploying a paid tier does not happen by
   itself*. `ARCHITECTURE.md` §7's distinction survives the change intact — *building*
   `packages/cloudflare/`'s source stays distinct from *deploying* it, and deployment stays a
   manual, separately-triggered act. **Changed:** `DEMO-04`'s row above and `HOST-11`.

**A second ruling of the same date, recorded here because it changes what Phase 29 can close.**
Autonomous execution may do everything in Phase 29 **except touch Cloudflare**: the package,
the datastore, the four guards and the build test that settles open question 1 all proceed
without an account. **Criteria 1 and 2 — the billing alert and the first deploy — stay open and
must be reported open.** A phase report that ticks either on work done locally has widened what
counts as passing, which is the one thing this ledger exists to prevent.

---

### Explicitly not in v2.0

| Item | Why it stays out |
|---|---|
| **Cold fan-out through a Cloudflare gateway/routing path** | The 50-subrequests-per-invocation cap is cumulative and non-refundable on close, while reuse on an already-open socket is free. Scoped out **unless a warm connection-pool design is budgeted into the milestone** — with one, it is in scope and must be load-tested against the cap; without one, building the fan-out path builds a ceiling nobody measured |
| **Leaderboards, team rankings, contribution ranking** | `PROJECT.md` excludes reputation markets outright, and a competitive ranking is a soft version of the same thing: it invites fabricated nodes and unattended always-on scripts left running purely to rank |
| **Any payment or cryptocurrency framing** | Out of scope per `PROJECT.md`. Browser compute demonstrably cannot be paid — the largest operators of the 2017-era in-page cryptocurrency scripts took single-digit dollars over months — and the framing reintroduces exactly the reputational shadow this project needs distance from |
| **Attentiveness-adaptive intensity ramping** | Ramping work up when the visitor is probably not looking is *precisely* the cryptojacking evasion pattern documented in the wild (arXiv:1808.09474): behaviourally indistinguishable from malware regardless of intent, and caught by the same antivirus and ad-blocker heuristics that killed the 2017-era scripts |
| **A single global on/off switch with no regional or cohort slice** | One bad region would take the whole cohort offline for volunteers whose region was never affected. `RUN-02` requires slices for this reason |
| **A native app, browser extension, or elevated-permission install** | Defeats the project's own core value — every visitor to a web page is a potential compute node — and reads as *more* suspicious to a security-conscious volunteer than an in-tab consent click |
| **Silent Battery Status API detection for a low-power pause** | Firefox removed that API in 2016 specifically as a fingerprinting vector and Safari never shipped it. A low-power pause is a preference the visitor states, not a property the page detects |
| **TEE tiers, zk proofs, S/Kademlia, secure aggregation** | Unchanged, and they live in `## v2 Requirements (deferred)` below — which, despite the name, is not this milestone. See the note under that heading |

---

## v2 Requirements (deferred)

**Clarified 2026-08-25.** This heading predates the v2.0 milestone and does not name it.
It was written when *v2* meant *someday, far future* — TEE tiers, zk proofs, S/Kademlia —
and the milestone now called **v2.0 "Open the Doors"** is a different thing entirely. The
two headings read alike and are not alike. Read this one as *beyond the current milestone*;
the milestone's own requirements are the `## v2.0 Requirements — Open the Doors` section
above. The heading is kept rather than renamed or deleted so that anything citing it still
resolves.

- Mergeable sketches (HyperLogLog, t-digest, Count-Min) for approximate holistic ops
- Secure aggregation and differential privacy over cross-owner partials
- TEE / confidential-computing backbone tier with remote attestation
- Native execution in Firecracker / Kata microVMs
- Delegated tree-coordination for single very large jobs
- S/Kademlia hardening (not implemented in js-libp2p — build, not configure)
- Cryptographic proofs of computation

---

## Out of Scope

- **Outside contributions** — sole authorship preserves the commercial license track
- **Incentives, payments, staking, reputation markets** — meaningless before
  capacity scaling is proven; browser compute demonstrably cannot be paid
- **Aqua-class choreography DSL** — Fluence archived exactly this; chain jobs by
  CID from outside instead
- **Key-partitioned all-to-all shuffle** — the expensive escape hatch; decomposable
  reduce covers the target workloads
- **Emulation fallback / container2wasm** — ~10x+ slowdown for kernel fidelity
  nobody has requested
- **Server-side WASM runtimes in the portable agent** (Wasmtime / WasmEdge / Marine)
  — would fork the agent and destroy the portability bet; build-pipeline only
- **WebTransport** — dial-only in js-libp2p, cannot listen, absent from Safari
- **Bulk data over the browser mesh** — WebRTC caps messages at 16 KiB and
  Chromium closes channels above 256 KiB; artifacts fetch over a gateway

---

## Traceability

All 72 v1 requirements are mapped, each to exactly one phase, plus the **10** v1.1-only
requirements below. See `.planning/ROADMAP.md` for phase goals and success criteria.

**Six rows were missing until 2026-08-08, and their absence was not cosmetic.** `SCHED-06`,
`NET-08`, `NET-09`, `NET-10`, `DATA-10` and `BENCH-07` were ticked in the checklist above with no
row here, and this preamble said "the 4 v1.1-only WIRE requirements" — which was true of what the
table held and false of what the milestone shipped. `acceptance-traceability.node.test.ts`
enforces *"`[x]` iff the traceability verdict begins `Done`"* **only over rows that exist**, so
those six checkboxes were unguarded: nothing would have noticed one regressing. Found by the v1.1
milestone audit's three-source cross-reference, which is the check that reads the checklist and
this table as two independent sources rather than one.

**CITATIONS IN THIS TABLE ARE ANCHORED ON SYMBOLS, NOT ON LINE NUMBERS — F-23, closed 2026-08-25.**
Audit finding F-23 (`.planning/phases/phase-18-discovery-capacity-placement/18-VERIFICATION.md`) recorded
that this file's `file:line` citations had drifted off their subjects, and left no destination because the
file was shared at the time. The repair adopted here is the one `.planning/ROADMAP.md` already took for the
same problem — ***cited by symbol, not by line, and that is the correction rather than the numbers***.
139 unique `path:line` citations were resolved against the tree; the drifted ones are re-cited by the symbol,
declaration, call site or quoted sentence they were always about, and the sound ones were left alone. Three
kinds of case are handled by a dated note instead of an edit, each named in the row it belongs to: a subject
that has left the tree (`CRYPTO-01`), a coordinate whose repair would move a measured figure (`AUTH-04`,
`CHURN-03`), and a row whose earlier layers are kept quotable rather than re-pointed (`NET-06`, `MR-06`).
**No verdict, checkbox, id or claim moved in this pass.** Where a citation still carries a line number it is
there because it still lands on its subject, or because it is a preserved wrong reading the surrounding
sentence corrects — and a bare `:NNN` shorthand is a reading of its own date, not a pointer.

| Requirement | Phase | Status |
|-------------|-------|--------|
| DET-03 | Phase 14 — Signed Artifact Resolution | Done — `guardModuleProvenance` (`core/src/executor/module-provenance.ts`) is composed innermost in both production node factories (`fabric-node.ts`, `browser-node.ts`, search `guardModuleProvenance(`), and `trustAnchors` is a **required** option on both, so no node can be built without stating whose modules it will run. A task with no `NameRecord`, one signed by an unpinned key, or one vouching for a different CID is refused before `inner.execute`; the refusal names the missing record or the offending key. Verified across a real spawned `bin/agent.ts` process (`signed-artifact.node.test.ts`, 5/5) and two real browser contexts (`two-tabs.e2e.test.ts`, 6/6) by 14-VERIFICATION.md |
| DET-05 | Phase 1 — Portable Kernel & Loopback Map Slice | Done |
| DET-06 | Phase 1 — Portable Kernel & Loopback Map Slice | Done |
| DET-07 | Phase 1 — Portable Kernel & Loopback Map Slice | Done |
| VER-01 | Phase 1 — Portable Kernel & Loopback Map Slice | Done |
| VER-02 | Phase 1 — Portable Kernel & Loopback Map Slice | Done — 2026-08-11, in two steps three days apart: the ceremony and its refusals first, marked *Partial* with the one open leg named, then the submitting leg that closed it, which is the last paragraph here. The ceremony that shipped under this id was minted and compared by the requestor itself, so its check was unconditionally true and both failure branches unreachable — measured 2026-07-30 over 1171 tests with no reach — and it was removed rather than relabelled because its nonce derived from three public values, so it hid nothing. **All three things that diagnosis said were missing are now present**, in `packages/core/src/job/commit-reveal.ts` and `@o2/net`'s `commit`/`reveal` wire kinds. The digest is computed by the **executor** (`serveAgent` draws the nonce, hashes its own answer and publishes `H(nonce ‖ moduleCid ‖ inputCid ‖ index ‖ resultCid)`), so the requestor compares a value it was given against one it derives from the revealed output — a comparison it did not author either side of. The nonce is 32 CSPRNG bytes, so the commitment hides. And there is a cross-node barrier: every commitment settles before any reveal is asked for. **The refusals are reachable and each was watched red**, which is the specific thing the last attempt could not say: `M75`–`M80` in `mutation-ledger.ts` replant the tautology, the derived nonce, the barrier, the serving node's committer pin, the wiring, and the shard binding, and all six were re-caught by `npm run test:mutations` with the recorded signature. The counterfactual is measured on one fixture rather than argued — with `submitJob`'s selection forced false the same plagiarising node comes back as **two agreeing replicas over a forged answer**, silently (`expected 2 to be 1`, `packages/net/src/commit-reveal-wire.test.ts`). **The serving leg is measured against a runnable entry point**: `packages/node/src/two-process.node.test.ts` runs both rounds across two spawned `bin/agent.ts` processes and names a replica that reveals what it did not commit to. **Scoped to the public tier, and the row says so rather than implying coverage it does not have**: `PROJECT.md`'s integrity table makes sovereign data owner-attested with the *aggregation* verified, so `submitJob` selects the ceremony for `label: 'public'` only and `serveAgent` refuses a sovereign `commit` by name — a ceremony over an owner's own two nodes resists nothing and would invite a reader to infer the independence `owner-domain` explicitly denies (VER-10). `executeCommitReveal` is reachable only through `submitJob`, which is deliberate and is the same shape VER-03 and VER-04 carry: the ceremony is how a job verifies, never a second way to run one, and `job-entry-points.node.test.ts` enforces that by keeping it off the barrel. **The submitting leg closed on 2026-08-11, which is what moved this to `[x]`.** Until then `bin/bench.ts` wrapped every executor in `timed`, whose returned literal carried `nodeId` and `execute` only, so a `RemoteExecutor` that arrived able to speak both rounds left unable to and every R≥2 rung took the comparison path. `timed` now forwards `commit` and `reveal` when the wrapped executor has them — structurally, because `isCommitting` is deliberately off the barrel, and the two cannot disagree in the direction that matters: when the forward happens the wrapper carries both functions and `submitJob`'s own predicate answers true of it. **The reading is the binary's, not a spec's**: the driver counts the rounds it forwarded and prints them per rung, and a `--quick` run reports `0 commits, 0 reveals` on each 1-node rung against `192 commits, 192 reveals` on every 2- and 4-node rung — 16 shards × 2 replicas × 6 runs, the R=1 zero being `submitJob` declining a ceremony that would bind an answer no peer could have copied. **What it costs was measured rather than asserted, and the figures it moved are named.** Six `--quick` runs per arm, interleaved, comparing each R=2 rung against the R=1 rung *of its own run* so the machine and the day cancel: on the real transport the n=2/n=1 makespan ratio goes 1.815 → 2.403 and the gross node-seconds ratio 3.870 → 5.141, both with disjoint sample ranges — about **+32%** on a redundancy-2 rung, one added round trip per replica per shard; on the in-process memory transport the effect is +0.4% to +6% at the median and is **not** separable from run-to-run variation at that sample size, which is what an added round trip over `MemoryNetwork` should look like. Stale as a result, and marked in place with the date and the reason rather than silently left standing: every rung at nodes ≥ 2 of both makespan tables in `BENCHMARK-RESULTS.md`, the connectivity tax and COST crossover derived from them, the skew reading, and the four `redundancy 2` rows of §4 in `docs/perf/prime-and-pi-benchmarks.md` — which the demo's Benchmarks surface transcribes and P9 holds, so those figures were annotated and not edited. The redundancy-1 speedup sweeps and the reduce tables are not on the changed path. **The demo page's set is a second thing and not a second instance of this gap**: `runPi`, `runColouring` and `runJob` lead with `node.signingExecutor`, an in-process executor of the submitting tab itself, so a tab-plus-one-peer job at R=2 falls through to the comparison — correctly, because a ceremony whose committer is the requestor's own process is the requestor minting and checking both sides, which is precisely the tautology the deleted ceremony was removed for, and because the local replica's answer never crosses the wire there is nothing for the one remote replica to copy. And the exclusion is per shard rather than per page: `submitJob` asks the question of the *chosen* executors, so on a tab with two or more peers the shards placement puts on two peers do take the ceremony while the ones it puts on the tab plus a peer do not — which is the same rule reading two different placements, not a surface that opted out |
| VER-03 | Phase 19 — Quorum Composition & Owner-Domain Attestation | **Done** — closed 2026-08-14 by repairing the RULE rather than the fixture; the paragraphs below are kept in order so the earlier verdicts stay readable. Marker corrected 2026-08-03 by 19-12, and the correction is about the label rather than about new work: *Built, not wired* asserts that nothing calls the mechanism, and 19-06 gave `composeQuorum` a production caller a wave earlier while this row went on saying otherwise. `composeQuorum` is reachable only through `submitJob`, and a public shard at redundancy two or more is placed on the member set it returns, so the shared-dependency rule decides a placement instead of sitting beside one. **The requirement is eclipse resistance and the mechanism is rule 2** — `sharedRelay` over the *chosen members*, not over the candidate pool; 19-02's implementation of the old *backbone-anchored* phrasing was retracted in `0314208` for keying on node kind, and `quorum.ts`'s header carried a flat *unimplemented* sentence for part of that day which was an overstatement scoped to the durability half alone, corrected in `2ddc9e5`. Pinned by `M39` (the rule asked of the pool: a pool on relay-1/relay-1/relay-2 passes and both drawn members hang off relay-1) and `M41` (the retracted node-kind rule reinstated). **Two halves stay open, and the second is why this is not `[x]`.** No default path reaches the gate: composition is attempted only when this requestor holds a certificate for every candidate, and **CORRECTED 2026-08-12 — this clause said *"every production submitter but `bin/bench.ts --discover` — off by default — builds its descriptors through `publicNodes`, which states it carries none"*, and the VER-09/VER-10 work falsified the exception.** The demo is a second submitter that does not go through `publicNodes`: `attestedNodes` (declared in `packages/browser/demo/main.ts`) replaced it at all three demo submit sites — `:799`, `:918` and `:1206` — and it does not default the field. It asks each peer for its records over the fabric's own RPC and verifies the answer with `verifyCertificate` against an anchor this tab held before any peer spoke (`:350`), on no flag at all. The sentence is quoted rather than deleted because it was true when written and the argument it belongs to is about which submitters can reach the composition gate. **What is true now leaves this half exactly as open, and the blocker is one step earlier than the old sentence put it.** A descriptor the demo builds carries a certificate only where this tab was itself enrolled, and no default path enrols: `autoStart` passes no `enrollment` and `main.ts` says so in its own words, in the comment beginning *"AUTH-01/04 — where to enrol, learned from the origin that served this page"*: *"A seed may publish where a joiner should knock; it may not publish who the joiner is"*, which is why `autoStart` *"still passes no `enrollment` and must not grow a parameter for one"*. So `n.certificate` is `null`, `peerCertificate` returns `'carries-no-certificate'` at `:322` before it asks anybody, and `BrowserNodeOptions` has no `trustedIssuers` field to pin an issuer with at all (`:291`). The blocker therefore moved from *the descriptor cannot carry a certificate* to *nothing a person can run issues one*, which is more specific and not smaller: composition is still attempted only where the requestor holds a certificate for every candidate, and on every default path it holds none. **Rule 2's across-process reading is CLOSED, and this row denied it until 2026-08-05 in three separate sentences that had each become false.** It read: *“rule 2 has no across-process reading at all”*, that 19-08's three executors *“are in-process `FabricNode`s started `listen: []`”*, and that *“`bin/agent.ts` passes `listen: ['/ip4/127.0.0.1/tcp/${port}']` unconditionally and can therefore only ever produce a `seed` with empty `relayIds`”*. All three describe the tree as it stood before 2026-08-04, and the first **contradicted the `M40` ledger entry this row cites in the same breath** — the ledger being the correct one, which is the direction a document is meant to be corrected from. What Plan 19-19 changed: `--port` no longer has a default, so *not passed* is different from *passed as `0`* and the listen list is built conditionally. An agent given `--relay-addr` and no `--port` binds nothing, signs `discoverability: 'via-relay'` naming its relay, and every address it holds is one the relay granted — read off a certificate a provider **process** signed, and asserted in both directions in `packages/node/src/quorum-agents.node.test.ts`. `M40` is the measurement that makes the one-relay fabric the case which carries rule 2, and it says so against its own interest: planted against the whole file, 1 failed and 3 passed, and the three that cannot redden are given no relay at all, so `sharedRelay` answers `null` on sight of a seed. A reader taking the three-operator fabric as evidence for rule 2 has taken a green that could not have gone red. **What stays open is the first half only**: no default path reaches the gate, per the paragraph above **CLOSED 2026-08-14, and by repairing the RULE rather than the fixture.** The plan of record was to qualify the demo page's candidate pool by block advertisement; that plan is REFUTED and the refutation is recorded because it was nearly built: the input block is created INSIDE `submitJob` after the pool is chosen, so nobody ever advertises it and the page would have gone permanently solo rather than empty-early. The actual defect was in `sharedRelay` itself. `relayIds` names relays by libp2p PEER ID while a quorum member is identified by `nodeKey`, an ed25519 public key — two spellings of one node that never compare equal — and a relay binds a socket so it enrols `seed`, whose dependency set reads empty. So when the relay every other member depended on was ITSELF a member, no comparison in `quorum.ts` could see it, and the composer reported a redundancy that one node's failure would erase. That is the sharpest instance of this requirement there is, and the rule was blind to it. `QuorumRules.peerIdOf` closes it, supplied by `submitJob` from descriptors it already holds, so core still imports no libp2p; with `peerIdOf` absent the predicate PROVABLY collapses to the old function, which is what keeps every pure-core caller on the rule it already read. Read on the surface a visitor gets, no flag: `not composed [shared-relay-dependency] — relay 12D3KooWDzGL… is itself a member of the quorum and every other member is discoverable only through it`. Planted red (`PLANTED_EXIT=1`) and restored by surgical inverse with `cmp` exit 0. |

The same file measures what the phase *did* change, as a count, which needs no calibration: a relay that issues certificates of its own while pinning a different provider performs **three full issuances for three askers and grants zero reservations** — every millisecond the provider spent produced an identity worth nothing. | AUTH-05 | Phase 19 — Quorum Composition & Owner-Domain Attestation | **Done** — closed 2026-08-03 by 19-09. 19-01 gave the per-owner grouping its first production caller: `discoverCandidates` fills `CandidateSet.replicaSets` from the certificates it just qualified, against the clock that qualified them, and `NodeDescriptor` carries each node's certificate instead of discarding everything but its user key. The consuming half that stayed open — *nothing puts a user key into `PlacementRequest.ownerId`* — is what 19-09 closed, in two places. `bin/agent.ts` derives a node's own clearance from the public half of its `--user-key`, refusing exit 2 if a passed `--owner-id` disagrees, so a node's clearance can no longer name an identity its certificate does not; and `CandidateOptions.dispatch` became a function of the node id, without which the chain a sovereign dispatch needs could name only one candidate's audience and discovery could authorise no sovereign placement at all. `packages/node/src/owner-domain-agents.node.test.ts` reads a replica set of two over real processes, `canVerifyWithinOwnerDomain` true, a third owner excluded `not-cleared-for-owner` by name, and a shard placed on the set |

| NET-03 | Phase 3 — Browser Tier & Backbone Relay | Partial — relay is browser-dialable, and **the AutoTLS half is no longer unmeasured**. Measured 2026-08-17 against `local-acme.ts`, a loopback RFC 8555 authority with a real UDP DNS zone and a PeerID-authenticating p2p-forge stand-in: `@ipshipyard/libp2p-auto-tls` orders, receives, installs and re-uses a certificate with none configured anywhere, and a TLS handshake validates the chain under the derived name. The old blocker sentence — *"AutoTLS needs a publicly reachable host"* — was a fact about **Let's Encrypt** restated as a fact about the mechanism. What remains is a hosting decision: the authority is not a public one, and this host has no interface that is not RFC 1918 or a ULA, so the dialable address is declared through `appendAnnounce` rather than bound. **RE-READ 2026-08-31 at 15 days outstanding, and the verdict is unmoved — recorded here because the half that moved is the half that tempts a tick.** All three witnesses were run rather than read: `auto-tls.node.test.ts` and `relaying.node.test.ts` 22 of 22, `seed-binary-join.e2e.test.ts` 2 of 2. What moved is the Cloudflare route, which stopped being source and became a deployment — the hosted node has answered since 2026-08-27 across eight tagged releases through `v2.0.0-rc.8` on one PeerId, its relay carries real reservations, and as of today it answers the fabric's rendezvous, so it introduces the peers it holds rather than merely holding them. On that route TLS is a commercial certificate terminated at the edge with nobody managing one, which reads like both clauses at once. It is not: the certificate requirement **does not arise** there rather than being met. What holds the row open is unchanged and is the whole of it — the authority `auto-tls.node.test.ts` orders from is still `local-acme.ts` and not a public one, and this host still has no interface that is not RFC 1918 or a ULA, so the address is still declared rather than bound |

| NET-05 | Phase 18 — Discovery, Capacity & Placement | **Done** — 2026-08-02, plan 18-11. Both halves. The *reading* half was already wired: capacity is derived from the live reservation store and printed by the seed. The *joiner* half is closed here — `bin/agent.ts` gains `--relay-addr` and is the **first production process ever to construct a `ReservationWatcher`**, which existed, was exported, and was reached only by two tests. `bin/seed.ts` now prints a dialable relay multiaddr, because it previously printed only HTTP join URLs and an operator wanting to point an agent at it had to read `seed-server.ts` — a criterion whose own configuration is unreachable without reading the source is guessed at, not configured. Measured in `packages/node/src/reservation-exhaustion.node.test.ts` across one real seed and three real agents: the first is granted a circuit, the second reports `at-capacity: RESERVATION_REFUSED` **by name**, and the third reports `unreachable`. **The second and third are the point** — both end with no circuit address and look identical from outside while demanding opposite responses, and the run separates them by having the refused joiner reach the very address that granted the first one moments earlier. **A production defect was fixed to get here**: `FabricNode.start` dialled relays with a bare `await`, inside `start` and therefore before the node existed, so an unreachable relay became an unhandled rejection in `bin/agent.ts` rather than a named refusal. The dial is now non-fatal and the failure is surfaced on `FabricNode.relayFailures`. Reddened by silencing the refusal report (the joiner never names it) and by making the dial fatal again (the third joiner exits 1). **A refusal arriving after startup is reported by the same path and is measured by nothing** |

| CHURN-02 | Phase 20 — Single Job Path, Ledger & Churn Resilience | **Done** — closed 2026-08-05, and this row carried the same false sentence `CHURN-01` did. `submitJob` duplicates a shard that has fallen behind its peers, takes the first answer, and — this is the half that is easy to omit — **still reads the copy that lost**, recording it as agreeing, disagreeing, failing or never answering rather than discarding it. Discarding is not a tidy: a losing copy that disagrees would turn redundant execution into majority-vote-by-race with a sample of one, which is why mutation-ledger `D2` is the load-bearing entry of the build and `D1` and `J1` sit either side of it (duplication happens at all; nothing is duplicated before `MIN_SAMPLES` completions give a median to compare against). **Measured across seven real spawned `bin/agent.ts` processes** by `packages/node/src/speculation-agents.node.test.ts`, which freezes a worker with a real signal and reads the on and off arms of **one** fabric with one job spec. | CHURN-04 | Phase 20 — Single Job Path, Ledger & Churn Resilience | **Done** — **BOTH HALVES RE-MEASURED 2026-08-25, and one of them was never false.** **(1) The lease control has a production entry point**, which this row's own later layer already records: `bin/agent.ts` declares `lease-ms`, validates it as a positive integer, refuses it without `--coordinate`, and spreads it into the spec handed to `submitJobWithEgress`, where `LeaseTable` receives it. **(2) The `30 s` is now a DEFAULT, not an invariant.** `DEFAULT_LEASE_MS` in `packages/core/src/lease.ts` still reads 30 000 ms and is still what an unset job gets, so the figure survives — what does not survive is any sentence saying nothing an operator runs can move it. The two-thirds renewal arithmetic below is unaffected. **(3) The clause saying `SubmitOptions` carries no lease-sizing field was correct when written and is still correct.** The field lives on `JobSpec`, and `submit.ts` says so against its own interest in the comment beside it. A re-measurement brief for this pass asserted the field was on `SubmitOptions`; both measurers refused that independently, so the wrong symbol never reached this file. **What this means for the F-23 note above**: its phrase putting the field “on its options” is the imprecise part, not the row's clause. closed 2026-08-18 on the clause the row itself finally identified, and everything below is kept because the substitution it records is the interesting part. **The split below is not the one this row used to claim.** It read *Built, not wired — runResilient has no caller*, which was wrong about the category: `LeaseTable.renew`, `shouldRenew` and `RENEW_AT` had no caller **anywhere**, including inside `runResilient`, so renewal had to be **built** and not wired. Renewal is granted **only on evidence the holder is still working**: the requestor offers the holder the same capacity slot key the dispatch took and is refused as a duplicate, which is positive evidence rather than a timer. `W5` plants the unconditional form and the run does not merely fail, it stops terminating — the fixture gives its virtual clock a horizon so an unbounded lease is a named failure rather than a hang. **The open leg is which production paths can renew at all**: the probe reuses `JobSpec.admit`, and `admit` is optional. **This clause read *"the only production submitter that supplies it is `bin/bench.ts --discover`, off by default"* and became false at `c1f95a2`**, which gave `demo/main.ts`'s `runColouring` an `admit` with no flag; it is quoted rather than deleted because it was true when written. **And supplying it did not make renewal reachable — measured 2026-08-16, the first full e2e run since that merge.** `rpcAdmission` asked the *submitting* node about itself over RPC, the transport refused to dial self, and every shard carried `unreachable: … Can not dial self` against the submitter's own id, so a two-tab job at `redundancy: 2` reached one replica and `job.complete` was false. Four e2e files and six cases went red — DEMO-01, NET-06 and VER-09/VER-10's three — none of which had changed. A two-arm run settled it on one host: `admit` present EXIT 1, `admit` removed EXIT 0, and a third arm at `probeTimeoutMs` 15 000 failed identically, so it was a refusal and not a budget. `rpcAdmission` now takes a `local` port and answers a self-addressed offer from it. **What stays open is therefore narrower than this row used to say and wider than the fix**: a default path now supplies `admit` and the probe reaches every node including the submitter, and **no spec drives a lease RENEWAL on a live tab fabric** — `packages/net/src/discovery.test.ts` witnesses the precondition, not the renewal. **RE-MEASURED 2026-08-18 and the box does NOT move; what changed is that the open leg can now be stated as an arithmetic fact instead of an absence.** `bin/agent.ts`'s new `--coordinate` leg is a **third** production submitter supplying `admit` — `rpcAdmission(node.rpc)` on a job dispatched to `--peer-addr` peers — so the count in the quoted clause above is again out of date in the good direction, and it reaches every candidate because this node's own id is absent from the descriptor set it builds, so the self-offer branch that broke the tab fabric on 2026-08-16 is never entered from here. **It still drives no renewal, and now the reason is a number rather than a gap in coverage**: `DEFAULT_LEASE_MS` is 30 000 ms and `RENEW_AT` is two-thirds of it, so a dispatch must be outstanding for **20 s** before `shouldRenew` can return true, while a colouring cube at `--coordinate-n 300` measured **~60 ms** on this host — a factor of ~330. A spec that drove a real renewal on a live fabric would therefore have to hold one task open for twenty seconds per arm, which is a workload decision and not a test that was forgotten. `packages/node/src/checkpoint-coordinator.e2e.test.ts` says this about itself in its own *What this file CANNOT redden on* block rather than leaving it to be inferred, so the precondition being wired on a fourth path is not mistaken for the renewal being read. **RE-READ 2026-08-18 AGAINST THE REQUIREMENT RATHER THAN AGAINST THE ROW, AND THE OPEN LEG IS A DIFFERENT ONE — this is the correction, and everything above is kept because it is true and because the substitution it made is the interesting part.** CHURN-04's own sentence is *"Task ownership is leased and **re-dispatched on lease expiry**"*. Every paragraph above holds this row open on **renewal**, which is the mechanism that prevents an expiry — the opposite leg — and renewal is named nowhere in the requirement. That substitution has stood since Phase 20. **What the requirement asks is not measured across real processes, and the reason is precise: no cross-process loss in this repository has ever been an `expired` one.** `packages/node/src/churn-agents.node.test.ts` — CHURN-01's spec, eight real spawned `bin/agent.ts` processes, `SIGKILL` — filters losses on a **disjunction**, `event.kind === 'expired' || event.kind === 'surrendered'`, and records at `:802` that **every loss it measures is a `surrender`**: a SIGKILLed socket closes well inside `RPC_TIMEOUT_MS`, so `submitJob` calls `leases.surrender(...)` and the deadline never bites. The file's own header rules out the other leg in the same breath (`:68-71`): nothing in it holds a lease as far as `RENEW_AT`, because every dispatch is bounded by `rpcTimeoutMs` far below 20 s. **So expiry-driven re-dispatch is fixture-only.** The single spec that drives it through `submitJob` at all is `packages/core/src/job/submit.test.ts:1460`, which reaches `expired` and `redispatches === 1` on a **virtual clock** whose only source of advancement is the module's own `sleep` — the same fixture that carries the renewal pair, and it states its own blind spot at `:1610-1611`. **And the knob that would close it is already named by the spec that cannot use it** (`churn-agents.node.test.ts:805-807`): an `rpcTimeoutMs` above the lease, or a peer behind a relay whose reservation outlives the process. Neither needs new mechanism. So the honest classification of this row changes from *a workload decision nobody has taken* to **MEASURING, and cheap** — one arm holding a dispatch open past `DEFAULT_LEASE_MS` across real OS processes, reading an `expired` event and the re-dispatch that follows it. So *no production entry point at all*, *no lease-sizing field* and *exactly 30 s* are each a claim a re-measurement pass owns. The paragraph is left exactly as written rather than half-corrected, because a citation repair that moves a claim is the failure this finding exists to prevent; the line number is dropped so nothing here reads as a live pointer.)* **And the count of `admit` suppliers in the paragraphs above is right by arithmetic and wrong by the 2026-08-15 owner ruling.** Three production sites supply it — `bin/agent.ts`, `demo/main.ts`, `bin/bench.ts`, each on its own `admit:` argument — but the third is behind `--discover`, and under *"it must work with no flag"* it is not shipped. The honest figure is **two**. **CLOSED 2026-08-18. Both halves of the paragraph above were built and measured, and the row's own two words — *re-dispatched on lease expiry* — are now read across real OS processes.** **(1) `leaseMs` reaches a production entry point.** `JobSpec.leaseMs` (`job/submit.ts`, search `readonly leaseMs`) is spread into the one production `LeaseTable` at `job/submit.ts`'s `new LeaseTable({`, and `bin/agent.ts --lease-ms <ms>` supplies it on the `--coordinate` leg. **It is a parameter and not a feature gate**, which the 2026-08-18 role-selector ruling requires to be argued rather than asserted: leasing, expiry and expiry-driven re-dispatch all run with the flag absent exactly as with it present — the flag moves a *duration*, the way `--coordinate-n` moves a problem size — and the default is `DEFAULT_LEASE_MS`, unchanged at 30 000, which is what still ships. So the ruling's test does not even apply: the capability is not behind the flag. **(2) The expiry is measured across processes.** `packages/node/src/lease-expiry.e2e.test.ts` — two cases, six spawned `bin/agent.ts` processes each — silences whichever executor is actually doing the work, found by polling both executors' own CPU (`ps -o pid=,time=`) rather than by a tuned sleep, and reads the coordinator's own lease history off its stdout. A stopped holder's twelve shards expire, are re-dispatched to the untried node and every one is answered: `expired` names the silenced peer id, each shard's `attempted` exceeds the generation that lapsed, each `ending` is `agreed`. Run 2026-08-18: **exit 0, 2 tests, `real 23.43 user 17.91 sys 3.14`** — a ratio of 0.90, which is what a spec whose wall clock is deliberately part *lease* is supposed to look like — and green on six consecutive standalone runs spanning 22.8–24.4 s. In the full `--project e2e` sweep the same day: **35 files, 225 tests, exit 0**; and the full `--project node` sweep is unmoved at **197 files, 2 961 passed, 1 skipped, exit 0**, which is `slow-specs`' own population saying this file did not touch it. **The reading is comparative, not absolute.** Same fixture at `--lease-ms 2000` and at `6000`: every expiry in the long arm waited longer than every expiry in the short one, so the interval follows the lease rather than a timer the machine chose. `heldMs` — that generation's own grant-to-expiry interval, on the requestor's clock — lands at `max(lease, ⅔ × lease + DEFAULT_PROBE_TIMEOUT_MS)`: ~3 330 ms and ~6 003 ms. **And which signal produces an expiry was measured rather than assumed, and the obvious answer is wrong.** A SIGKILLed holder on this fabric also produces `expired`, not `surrendered`, with `heldMs` at the lease exactly. `churn-agents.node.test.ts:795-806`'s surrender follows from *its own* `rpcTimeoutMs` of 10 000 sitting below a 30 000 ms lease — the RPC budget reports, not a socket close — which is precisely the condition that file named at `:805-807` as what would move the loss to `expired`, reached here from the other side by putting the lease below the budget. What the signal does change is the renewal probe: a stopped socket makes it time out, a closed one makes it fail at once. That is a different sentence from this requirement's, which names expiry and not renewal, and it is recorded here so the substitution cannot be made a third time |
| CHURN-05 | Phase 20 — Single Job Path, Ledger & Churn Resilience | **Done** — closed 2026-08-05, same false sentence as `CHURN-01` and `CHURN-02`. `JobResult` carries a `coverage` field typed as a named union — `CoverageReport | 'defines-no-owners'` — and the union is the load-bearing part rather than the report: `coverageOf` treats an empty owner set as **not** complete, so a bare report would have made every public job in this repository print `covered: 0/0 owners — PARTIAL (no owners were expected)` on every benchmark rung. `O2` pins the union and `O7` pins the display site, whose observed failure text is that sentence on the driver's own stdout. An owner counts as covered when **all** its shards landed and not on its first, which is the whole difference between a coverage report and a participation tally — `O1`. The owner set is derived from the job's own shards rather than from the owners of its nodes, so an owner with an unplaceable shard is still expected and correctly missing — `O4`, and that derivation is what let this phase avoid a five-site required `JobSpec` field. **Measured across real spawned `bin/agent.ts` processes** by `packages/node/src/coverage-agents.node.test.ts`: one owner's process stopped mid-fabric gives `covered: 2/3` naming that owner, against a `3/3` control on the same fabric, plus a partial-owner arm that reads `1` where a first-shard rule would read `2` |
| CHURN-06 | Phase 20 — Single Job Path, Ledger & Churn Resilience | **Done** — closed 2026-08-05, same false sentence as the four above. A speculative duplicate of a sovereign shard can only land on that shard's owner's own executable nodes, and the reason it holds is structural rather than defensive: `speculativeCandidates` routes through `eligibleNodes`, the same function both placers call as their first act, and the pool it is given is the **gate's** pool rather than the job's candidate set — so a composed quorum is not silently widened by a duplicate either. Where an owner has no spare node there is no branch that could do anything but wait. `D3` is the entry that makes this a reading rather than an argument, and it is planted at the level where it can fail: widening `speculativeCandidates`' *input* cannot fail, because it re-runs the gate on whatever it is handed, so the mutation bypasses the call altogether — the shape a re-dispatch written for liveness alone would take. **Measured across seven real spawned `bin/agent.ts` processes** by `packages/node/src/speculation-agents.node.test.ts`, which carries both arms in **one** job: an owner with two nodes whose sovereign shard may be duplicated onto its own other node, and an owner with one node whose shard may not be duplicated at all |
| BROW-01 | Phase 9 — Public Demo, Consent UX & Disclosure Gate | **Done — and it stayed Done through twelve days of a FALSE disclosure, which is worth recording against this row rather than only in the commit that fixed it.** BROW-01 says a visitor gives explicit informed consent **before any compute begins**. The wire landed in Plan 20-02 and the reading landed later, in a file this row never named. What is measured: both node factories build a real `StartOutcomeLedger` and **record their own start row into it at construction**, which is the half that is easy to omit and fatal to omit — `serveAgent` records only what a peer told it, so without the own row two tabs hand each other back their own rows and `mergeOverlapping` pins every merged report at 1 forever. `L1` is the entry that pins it and `L2` pins the hook itself; `L3` pins the wire answer, whose observed failure is a merged report that no longer carries a browser family the asking node has no expression to produce. The demo's own deferral of this — behind `MAX_REPORTED_COUNT` and `isStartBrowserLabel` — is spent, both bounds are enforced in `parseCounts`, and `L4` and `L5` are what stop either being loosened. **The row said two things were open. The first is closed and this row denied it for days; the second is open, is outside the criterion, and is carried here in full rather than absorbed.**

*The reading.* The sentence replaced here said no tab had yet been shown displaying counts it could only have obtained from a peer, and attributed the gap to Plan 20-06 having recorded no plant, no span and no exit code. `packages/node/src/peer-ledger.e2e.test.ts` exists and quotes criterion 5 verbatim in its own header. **Re-measured 2026-08-11 on the `e2e` project: exit 0, 7 tests.** Five tabs across three engines and a Node relay; the load-bearing assertions read `#report`'s `textContent` **off the live page** rather than a returned object, which is the `M37` distinction one tier down. The case that carries it — *shows, on screen, tally rows for two browser families the reading tab is not* — has the chromium tab display a `firefox` row and the firefox tab display a `chromium` one, plus the Node tier's `other` row arriving over the relay: a label `currentBrowserLabel()` cannot compose from this tab's own `navigator.userAgent`, so it **cannot have been produced locally**, which a count above 1 could always have been. Three engines is what makes that measurable at all — three tabs of one engine share a family label and `mergeOverlapping` takes the maximum per key, so a single-engine fixture cannot tell a merged report from a local one however many tabs it opens. The `unreliable` small-sample marker is asserted **present** rather than worked around, and the declining tab's row is asserted to reach no peer and no screen (BROW-01).

*The row-count bound, unclosed and stated with its arithmetic, because a limit recorded as a defence is how the next reader stops looking.* `parseCounts` caps each entry's magnitude (`MAX_REPORTED_COUNT`, 65 536) and validates each label (`isStartBrowserLabel`) but not the array length, and `StartOutcomeLedger`'s `#counts` map grows by one key per distinct `(browser, result)` a node is ever told about. NET-08's 8 MiB frame ceiling implies a crossover at **191 099 rows** at 43.90 encoded bytes per row. **Measured 2026-08-11: no honest bound binds tighter than that ceiling.** The truthful key space is `BROWSER_FAMILIES` (5) × labels admitted by `BROWSER_LABEL` × results (1 `started` + 6 `START_FAILURES` = 7). `MAJOR_DIGITS` is 4 and the pattern is `\d{1,4}`, so 11 110 version forms plus the bare family — 5 × 11 111 × 7 = **388 885 keys**, twice the frame ceiling. Refusing leading zeros, which no composer in this build emits, gives 5 × 10 000 × 7 = **350 000** — still above it. A bound that would bind requires narrowing `MAJOR_DIGITS`, which is a **wire-format decision with a disclosure dimension** (the coarseness of the label is what the sovereignty promise about it rests on), not a parser fix. So the ceiling that exists is NET-08's, it is per frame rather than per ledger, and this is where that is written down. **It is outside Phase 20 criterion 5**, which is what this box is scored against; a reader who thinks it belongs inside should clear the box rather than assume it was overlooked, and the shape it would take if minted is `EGR-01`'s — its own id, rather than a fourth deferral inside somebody else's row |
